#10678. When Should You Remove NAT from an On-Prem Kubernetes Network?

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

In an on-premises Kubernetes environment, removing NAT can make the network simpler to inspect, reduce translation overhead, and let the data center route directly to workloads. But direct routing is not automatically the right choice.

The decision depends on what the organization controls and what it needs from the network. A team that owns the physical fabric, has enough address space, and is comfortable operating BGP may benefit from making pod networks directly routable. A smaller environment, a team without control of the underlay, or a cluster that benefits from network abstraction may be better served by NAT or an overlay.

Kubernetes is also changing the ground under these decisions: kube-proxy is set to drop IPVS support starting with version 1.43, with nftables as the replacement. For on-prem teams already reviewing their service data path, it is a good moment to ask whether NAT still belongs in it.

So the useful question is not how to eliminate NAT, but when the operational benefits of doing so justify the additional routing responsibility.

NAT solves a real problem. It allows an internal network to use its own address space without requiring the surrounding infrastructure to know how to reach every endpoint inside it. This is especially useful when you don’t control the underlying network.

However, an on-premises data center is different. When you control both the Kubernetes cluster and the surrounding physical network, you can make Kubernetes networks directly routable.

In our case, removing NAT had practical and architectural benefits. It improved network performance, reduced CPU load on the nodes, and removed the risk of conntrack tables filling up on Kubernetes hosts. A directly routed L3 model also works well for traffic balancing when a node has two uplinks.

This does not mean eliminating NAT from the data center entirely. NAT can remain at the boundary of the on-premises network, for example on hardware firewalls. Inside the data center, however, Kubernetes networks can be directly routable, allowing the physical network to reach workloads using the same routing mechanisms it already uses elsewhere.

In our implementation, BGP connects the Kubernetes network to the data center fabric: BIRD runs on the nodes and advertises pod CIDRs and selected service addresses, while kube-router manages Kubernetes networking. We described this implementation in more detail in our earlier article on building a routable on-prem

Kubernetes cluster with BGP and ECMP. The important point for this decision is not the particular stack, but the requirement it creates: the surrounding network must be able to accept and route Kubernetes prefixes safely. If the organization cannot or does not want to make the cluster part of its routing domain, keeping NAT or an overlay is usually the simpler choice.

Removing NAT alone does not make a network resilient. If every node still depends on one switch, one NIC, or one uplink, direct routing simply exposes the same physical single point of failure more clearly.

The resilience benefit appears when the physical topology supports multiple independent paths. In our setup, nodes connect to independent data center switches and ECMP can use equivalent routes across those paths. If one path disappears, traffic can continue over the other.

That distinction matters when deciding whether direct routing is worth the effort. Organizations with redundant uplinks and switches can integrate Kubernetes into an already resilient routed fabric. If the underlay itself has no path redundancy, removing NAT should not be presented as a high-availability improvement: the physical failure domain still dominates the design.

Our stack is one example of how direct routing can be implemented, not a default recommendation for clusters that remove NAT. We chose kube-router because it supports a minimal installation as a binary and systemd service and covers the networking functions we need for this L3 design. BIRD handles route advertisements to the surrounding network.

Other environments may reasonably choose Calico for conventional L3 routing or Cilium when an eBPF-based dataplane and its additional capabilities are useful. The choice of CNI and routing daemon is secondary to the architectural decision: first decide whether exposing Kubernetes prefixes to the physical network makes operational sense, then choose the implementation that fits the team’s requirements.

A directly routed Kubernetes network is not automatically better than an overlay or NAT-based architecture. Each model solves a different operational problem.

NAT is a reasonable choice when the cluster needs to remain isolated from the surrounding address plan, when pod addresses cannot be routed through the data center, or when the infrastructure team does not control the upstream network. It also keeps Kubernetes-specific prefixes out of the physical routing domain, which can be valuable for smaller environments where direct visibility into every pod is not important.

An overlay is useful when portability and abstraction matter more than exposing the physical packet path. It lets Kubernetes networking remain largely independent of the underlay and can simplify deployment across heterogeneous infrastructure. The tradeoff is another layer to inspect during troubleshooting and, depending on the implementation, encapsulation overhead.

Direct routing makes the most sense when the organization owns the physical network, has address space available for pod CIDRs, and has operators comfortable with BGP and route filtering. It becomes more attractive when preserving source addresses, using standard network troubleshooting tools, predictable performance, or integrating the cluster with redundant data center paths are important. At a larger scale, those operational benefits can justify the extra coordination between Kubernetes and network teams.

The broader lesson is not that every Kubernetes cluster needs BGP or that NAT should disappear everywhere. It is that Kubernetes has matured enough that we should stop automatically treating its network as a black box.

As more production and AI workloads move onto Kubernetes, network architecture becomes part of application reliability. In an on-premises data center, BGP and ECMP give us the option to use the resilience already available in the physical network rather than building another isolated networking layer on top of it.

Sometimes the best way to make Kubernetes networking more robust is not to add another abstraction, but to remove one. But only when the surrounding infrastructure and the team operating it are ready to take responsibility for the routing that abstraction previously hid.

If most of those answers are yes, direct routing is worth serious consideration. If not, NAT or an overlay may be the more resilient operational choice—not because it is technically more advanced, but because it better matches the environment.

#10673. 7 Best Accounts Receivable (AR) Software Platforms for Automating Invoice-to-Cash

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The best accounts receivable software for your company is the layer that closes the stage where your cash leaks. If buyers settle from an emailed link and outreach starts late, a reminders tool covers most of it. If customers prefer to pay online by card or ACH, add a payments network with a customer portal. If clients require invoice uploads to Coupa or Ariba and clear multiple invoices with one blank-memo wire, you need an AR automation layer on top of the ERP. Every platform listed here serves B2B businesses invoicing other companies, excluding consumer payment apps and collection agencies.

Our source material is each vendor's public product, integration, security and pricing pages, plus G2 reviews, as they stood in November 2026.

About three in five US suppliers let business clients pay within 30 days, per Atradius's 2026 US payment practices survey (September 2026). Most overdue invoices settle within a month past due, so funds arrive roughly two months post-billing. The same survey notes US enterprises often lose 1% to 2% of B2B invoices to bad debt. Software that shortens this window performs four main functions:

What invoice-to-cash software does not handle is core billing operations. Billing schedules, revenue recognition, and GL accounting remain within the ERP. Contract-first platforms initiate workflows from signed agreements and map customer billing prior to collection efforts; invoice-first applications pick up after ERP invoice generation.

Most finance stacks have four layers: the ERP that creates the invoice and holds the books, a payments network, a reminders tool, and an AR automation layer working the steps in between.

The ERP covers the last row, and a reminders tool covers timing. The rows in between depend on reading what a customer or a portal sent, which is AR automation work. That share keeps growing. Paper checks fell to 26% of B2B payments in 2025, per the AFP 2025 Digital Payments Survey as reported by Nacha, and FedNow settled about 2.5 times as many payments in Q3 2026 as a year earlier, per Federal Reserve FedNow statistics. Heading into 2027, more money arrives over electronic rails while its remittance detail lands somewhere else.

Layer: AI-native AR automation, for Series A to Fortune 500 finance teams

Published pricing: Pricing scales with business volume and isn't a percentage of collections

Leak it closes: Unapplied cash first, then stalled replies and portal rejections.

Layer: AR automation with built-in payments, sold as Invoiced by Flywire

Leak it closes: Cash application, where CashMatch AI handles wires that pay several invoices at once and payments that arrive without remittance.

Leak it closes: Invoice delivery into buyer portals, through 260+ AP portal integrations run from one interface.

Layer: AR automation layer with AI agents, on the ERP

Leak it closes: Customer replies that carry a promise-to-pay date or a dispute.

Layer: Payments network with customer portal

Leak it closes: Payment acceptance, through a customer portal for paying and disputing invoices online.

Layer: Reminders tool with AI reply drafts, on Xero and QuickBooks

Published pricing: Essentials $69 a month; Pro $99 to $799 a month; Custom from $999 a month

Leak it closes: Replies that sit unanswered at small and midsize companies.

Published pricing: US $259, $779 or $1,169 a month by annual revenue band; annual billing saves 10%

Leak it closes: Follow-up timing, with reminders sent from your own email address on a schedule that escalates.

On Xero or QuickBooks with customers paying from the emailed link, the leak tends to be timing, and a reminders tool covers it. Once W-9 requests and payment questions fill your team's week, the replies need a different layer.

Portal rejections and lump-sum wires put the leak in the table's middle rows, where an AR automation layer on the ERP fits. In a demo, we'd ask each vendor to show how fast its software flagged a rejected portal invoice, then to apply a multi-invoice wire with a blank memo live.

If your team still keys card numbers or chases ACH details, start with the payments layer. A payments network with a customer portal gives buyers stored methods and autopay. Unless the vendor says otherwise, it won't read replies or file into buyer portals, so plan an AR layer for those stages.

Whichever of the best accounts receivable software platforms you pick, it sits on top of the ERP. Four steps keep the books intact:

#10667. Best Ecommerce Platforms for Developers: Six Options and the Layers Each Leaves to You

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picking an ecommerce platform is really a decision about which layers you want to own: the storefront, the catalog and order logic, the payment flow, and the hosting underneath. Every platform below draws that line in a different place. If you have already settled the front-end question with a comparison like SitePoint's Astro vs Next.js guide, the next question is what sits behind it. Prices are from each vendor's pricing page as of November 2026

Shopify is the fully managed option. Basic costs $29 a month with card rates from 2.9% plus 30 cents, and a 2% fee applies to orders processed through a third-party gateway on that plan. Developers get two routes: Liquid themes, or Hydrogen, Shopify's headless toolkit, where your framework owns routing and rendering and Hydrogen supplies the Storefront API client and cart. Templates exist for React Router on Oxygen and for Next.js on Vercel. Plus starts at $2,300 a month.

Whop is a business platform for taking payments and sending payouts, and it sits under a storefront you have already built rather than replacing it. The Checkout element runs in the browser with no server code and no API key on the page. You pass your own order ID as metadata, Whop copies it onto the payment, and your server fulfills from a signed webhook that follows the Standard Webhooks spec. Domestic cards cost 2.7% plus 30 cents with no monthly fee, buyers see payment methods chosen by their location across 195 countries, and the same balance pays out in over 200 countries, including to affiliates or sellers you onboard. Whop's own guide shows how to integrate a checkout API into a Next.js storefront end to end. The trade-off is that there is no catalog or shipping engine; you collect address fields at checkout and trigger fulfillment from the webhook.

WooCommerce is a free plugin on WordPress you host yourself, so you own updates, security, and performance. Its payment gateway, WooPayments, charges US merchants 2.9% plus 30 cents per card transaction, 1.5% more for international cards, 2% for currency conversion, and $25 per dispute. Everything is PHP hooks and filters, which makes it the natural pick for agencies already living in WordPress and the wrong pick for anyone who does not want to run a server.

BigCommerce hosts the back end and lets you bring any front end. Core is $29 a month billed annually, capped at $30,000 in trailing annual sales, and plans auto-upgrade as volume grows. There are no platform fees when you use an embedded payment provider, and a 2% fee on Core if you use another one. Catalyst, its reference storefront, is built on Next.js, React Server Components, and the Storefront GraphQL API, and deploys to Vercel.

Medusa is an open-source commerce framework in Node.js and TypeScript, with an MIT-licensed core and a commercial enterprise edition. SitePoint covered it when it was still a young Shopify alternative; it now has over 37,000 GitHub stars. Self-hosting is free. Medusa Cloud starts at $29 a month for development, $99 for launch, and $299 for scale, with no percentage of sales, and the company says you can export your data and self-host at any point.

Saleor is a GraphQL-native, API-only commerce platform written in Python and released under the BSD-3 license. Self-hosting is free. Saleor Cloud starts at $1,599 a month for up to $200,000 in monthly sales, with 0.8% on orders above that, and $3,999 a month at the next tier. It is built for teams with a dedicated engineering function and a large catalog, which is exactly who should pay that price.

Own only the storefront, and Shopify or BigCommerce will carry the rest. Own everything, and Medusa or Saleor give you a back end you can read. Already on WordPress, and WooCommerce is the obvious fit. Already have a storefront in Next.js or Astro and only need the money to move, and Whop is the shortest path. Whichever you pick, the checkout is where disputes start, so log what SitePoint's friendly fraud guide for developers recommends from day one.

#10662. Find Orphan Pages by Comparing Your Sitemap with an Internal Crawl

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A page can return 200 OK, exist in an XML sitemap, and still be practically invisible to anyone browsing the site.

You do not need a full commercial crawler to find the obvious cases. A small Node.js script can compare two things:

Anything present in the first set but missing from the second deserves investigation.

You now have URLs that the application publishes through its sitemap but that your HTML crawl could not reach.

The page might be intentionally isolated. It might exist for a campaign. Navigation might depend on JavaScript. Your crawler may have missed authentication or pagination.

Should a normal visitor reasonably be able to discover this page from another page on the site?

Google recommends using standard crawlable elements with resolvable href values for links that should be discoverable. Google's guidance on crawlable links

That is also good web architecture independent of SEO.

Internal links should not exist merely to move ranking signals around.

They should help users move between related tasks.

A technical guide might lead to a product page. A product page might link back to the configuration guide. A category page should make important child resources discoverable without requiring a user to know the exact URL.

This is why on-page SEO and internal linking are closely connected to site architecture. Good internal links clarify relationships for both visitors and crawlers.

At a broader level, crawlability is one part of technical SEO and search visibility. A page that nobody can reliably discover is difficult to evaluate regardless of how strong its content may be.

If an important URL appears disconnected because navigation is created only after client-side JavaScript runs, the script has surfaced a real architectural fact.

You can then decide whether that is acceptable.

If your application genuinely requires rendered-DOM crawling, replace the fetch-and-Cheerio layer with Playwright.

Do not make the simple crawler pretend to be something it is not.

The best output from this tool is not an "SEO score."

It is a concrete question for the development team:

This page exists. The sitemap knows about it. Why can't a normal HTML crawl reach it?

#10657. Power BI MCP server: how to brief an AI agent to build accurate measures

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Power BI, Microsoft's reporting and dashboard tool, now lets an AI agent work on the data model behind a report. If you ask one to add a year-on-year revenue measure (a measure is Power BI's name for a saved calculation) with Microsoft's MCP server connected, it can write the formula and save it to the model without you opening the formula bar. When we tried this on a sample model, an agent inspected the model, wrote the measure, tested it and saved it in under four minutes.

The agent did a careful job, but its formula didn't follow the same rules the finance team uses to count revenue. Nobody had told it what "revenue" meant in this business, so it had to decide for itself. We then gave another agent a short brief that set out finance's rules for what counts as revenue and which date to count it by. The agent wrote the formula from those rules, and the result matched finance's figure exactly. The two results show how much difference it makes in how you brief an AI agent to build accurate measures.

We wanted to find out what an agent can see in a Power BI model and how to brief it so the measure matches the business's own definitions, with the setup arranged so that every change can be checked. Developers will know the principle from agents that write SQL or API code, where the brief ends up doing the job of a specification.

Because MCP (Model Context Protocol) is an open standard, the same server can be used from different AI clients, GitHub Copilot in VS Code and Claude among them, although some clients need extra sign-in setup for the hosted version.

Power BI has two current MCP servers, plus an older one, the Power BI Consumption MCP server, kept for existing integrations. The first of the current pair is the Power BI Authoring MCP server, which you may still see under the name of its package, powerbi-modeling-mcp. It lets an agent create and change the objects inside a semantic model, which is the layer of a Power BI report that holds the tables, the relationships between them and the calculations, kept separate from the charts on the page. It comes in two forms. The hosted version, still in preview, needs nothing installed. The local version is generally available and runs on your own machine, which makes it the one to use when the model is a file you hold yourself, whether that's open in Power BI Desktop, the free Windows app used to build reports, or saved as a project folder. Macs aren't supported by the local version, so on a Mac it has to be the hosted one, which works only with models published to Microsoft's cloud (a Fabric workspace).

The second is the Fabric IQ MCP server. Fabric IQ is part of Microsoft Fabric, the wider data platform that Power BI now sits within. It gives an agent read-only tools for finding published reports and models and running queries against them, within the permissions already set, so the agent can answer questions about the data but can't change a model.

Which one to use follows from the job. Anything that changes a model goes through the authoring server, whether that's a new measure, a relationship or a set of descriptions, and it has to be the local version if the model is a Desktop file or a project folder under source control. Fabric IQ is for agents that only need answers from models other people maintain.

An agent connected to a semantic model can see its metadata (table and column names, data types, relationships, existing measures and any descriptions someone has written) and can run queries against the data. Everything it produces is inferred from those clues. A model that has served a business for years carries names that made perfect sense to the people who built it, and a column called Amt on a table called Sales_v2 tells an agent far less than it tells them.

Names carry a lot of the meaning. A column called Amount could hold the value with VAT (the UK's sales tax) or without, before credit notes or after, and an agent asked for "revenue" has to pick one. If the model holds several revenue measures built for different reports, the agent has to choose between them, and the one it picks may have been built for a different purpose.

The formulas depend on context as well. Power BI measures use a syntax of their own called DAX, which stands for Data Analysis Expressions. A DAX result shifts with whatever the person viewing the report has filtered or clicked on, which is known as filter context. A developer might think of a measure as a function that reads implicit state set by its caller. The same measure can be right as a single total on a card and need different handling once it's split by product in a table or filtered by a date slicer (the on-page date filter), depending on how CALCULATE (the DAX function that changes which filters apply) is used. Which behaviour is correct depends on how the business wants the number to respond, and that's something the agent has to be told.

Relationships work the same way. Plenty of models have more than one date column on the sales table, say order date and dispatch date, with only one relationship active at a time. A DAX measure uses the active relationship unless it's written to switch to the other one, and the agent has no way of knowing which date your monthly reporting runs on unless something tells it.

In each case the agent can write a valid measure. Which of two valid measures matches the business's own definition is the part it can't see from the model.

To see this in practice, we built a small sales model in a Power BI Desktop file, working through the server itself, with invented data: 5,266 order lines, with order dates from January 2025 to October 2026. We gave the Sales table an Amt column holding each line's value after discount with 20% VAT added, next to LineTotal and Discount columns that excluded VAT, and an existing [Revenue] measure that summed Amt. There were two date columns, with the active relationship to the Date table on dispatch date and the order date relationship inactive. Orders stop at the end of October 2026, which made the current year a part year, a deliberate test. Nothing in the model had a description. In this made-up business, finance reports revenue net of VAT and discounts, by order date.

On 12 November 2026 we connected an AI agent (Anthropic's Claude Fable 5.2) to version 1.0.1 of the local server through a small Node.js script that speaks MCP over stdio. In all three runs the agent had the server's tools only, without Microsoft's powerbi-authoring plugin, which adds modelling guidance on top of them. It was also told that nobody was available to answer questions, so each measure was saved exactly as the agent wrote it, with no review in between. The first prompt was one sentence: "Add a year-on-year revenue measure."

Before writing anything, the agent listed the tables, relationships and columns, read the existing measure, sampled some rows and checked the date ranges. It worked out for itself that 2026 was a part year and limited the comparison to the same dates in 2025, which avoided a misleading result of -6.7%. From 15 sample rows it also spotted that Amt looked like the net figure plus 20%.

The measure it saved reused the existing [Revenue] measure and followed the active relationship. This is the core of it, with the date handling and blank checks left out. CurrentDates is defined in those lines as the dates up to the last order:

It returned growth of 21.5% for 2026, which is correct for revenue counted by dispatch date. On finance's definition, by order date, the answer is 18.6%, and the whole difference came from that choice of date. The VAT question made no difference to a percentage, because Amt was already net of discount and every line carried the same rate, although it would have overstated any figure in pounds by 20%.

We reset the model and repeated the run with a fresh agent, which made the same two choices and returned the same 21.5%. Both choices were reasonable readings of the model as it stood, and the DAX was sound. In their reports, both agents flagged the date basis and the VAT treatment as open questions, and both worked out that an order-date basis would give 18.6%. That's what a good analyst would do, and with those two answers they would have had everything they needed.

Think of a capable analyst on their first morning, someone who knows DAX well but has never seen your business before and knows nothing (or not a lot) about it. You wouldn't say "add a revenue measure" and walk off. You'd tell them which table holds the trusted figures, whether revenue includes VAT, which date drives the reporting calendar, and what they shouldn't touch. An agent needs that briefing for the same reasons, and getting good work out of it draws on the skills you'd use with any new starter. Developers do the same thing when they write a good ticket. A request for a measure is a requirement, and it needs the same definitions and constraints, plus a way to check the result.

Before you send a prompt, ask yourself whether you could do the task properly if you were given only the words in that prompt and the model itself. If the answer is no, then the agent is in the same position, and it will make its own assumptions.

For the detailed run we cleared the earlier measure from the model and gave a fresh agent this brief:

This time the measure summed LineTotal minus Discount and used USERELATIONSHIP, the DAX function that switches a calculation to an inactive relationship, to work from order date. It returned 18.6% from that one brief, with no follow-up prompt, which is the figure we had calculated separately from the source data. This is the full measure as the agent saved it:

Each line of the brief answered a question the agent would otherwise have had to settle alone. It also made some sensible decisions the brief hadn't covered. It left the grand total blank, for instance, where a plain total across both years would have shown a meaningless growth figure of 151.2%. The final line of the brief, asking to see the DAX before anything is saved, is there so that a person can see choices like that. In the test we approved that step in advance, to keep the run unattended.

One detail needs adjusting before this measure is reused on other data. Its guard checks that each comparison period starts on or after the first order date, which works here because the sample data has an order on 1 January 2025. When we swapped that date for 2 January 2025 in a copy of the measure, January 2026 and the 2026 row came back blank. Comparing against the first date in the Date table, as the first agent's measure did, brought the 2026 figure back, since the Date table starts on 1 January. That version doesn't confirm that sales exist for the whole comparison period, though, so the right check depends on whether the business wants growth shown only where a complete prior period exists. That's a decision for whoever reviews the measure.

We ran the one-sentence prompt twice and the detailed brief once, all with the same AI model and one small set of data, which is enough to illustrate the pattern but too little to say how often it happens.

Good briefs help, but you'll be writing the same definitions into prompts again and again unless they live in the model itself. Descriptions on tables, columns and measures are part of the metadata the agent reads, so filling them in is one of the cheapest ways to put business definitions in front of it every time. In our test, where nothing had a description, both agents given the one-sentence prompt noted that nothing confirmed what Amt contained. A description such as "Net sales value in GBP, excluding VAT, after credit notes" stays in the model, where the agent reads it without anyone retyping it.

Naming deserves the same attention. Spelling out abbreviated column names, hiding technical keys that no report needs, gathering measures into a single measures table, and removing measures that are no longer used all give the agent fewer things to choose between. These are the same habits that make a model easier for a new colleague to pick up, so asking someone outside the team to find a figure in it is a fair test of the result.

Microsoft has also added a set of Prep data for AI features (in preview) to Power BI Desktop and the service. They let you choose which fields Copilot, Microsoft's AI assistant, should consider and write AI instructions that explain your business terms, with verified answers available for common questions. They're aimed at Copilot, but deciding and writing down your definitions is the same work whichever AI tool reads them.

An agent working through the authoring server has the same access as the person signed in, so anything that person can change, the agent can change. Sign in with an account that has no more access than the job needs, and take a copy of the model first, as you would before any bulk edit.

Start with read-only mode. The local server takes a --readonly flag that blocks all write operations, which is the right setting while you're learning how the agent behaves on your models. When we tried to create a measure in this mode, the server refused and said it was running read-only. In VS Code, the configuration looks like this (other clients use a similar structure, sometimes under an mcpServers key instead of servers):

You'll need Node.js installed for the npx command. The server won't run other tools until its licence terms (the EULA) have been accepted. Once you've read them, you can either tell the agent to accept for you or add --accepteula to the args list.

The --readonly flag belongs to the local server. On a Mac the route is the hosted server and a copy of the model in a Fabric workspace, once a Fabric administrator has switched on the tenant setting that allows the MCP endpoint, and there the copy is the safeguard. For an enforced limit, sign in with an account that holds only Build permission on the model, which lets the agent read and query it and nothing more.

Read-write is the default, so check how confirmation behaves in the version you install. Version 1.0.1 skips confirmation prompts by default, which the project's README hasn't caught up with, and has a --require-confirmation flag to turn them on. The flag is listed in the server's own --help output. In our runs every edit went through without a prompt. When we added the flag to the args list, the server asked for confirmation before the first write, with a message beginning "Are you sure you want to perform operations that will modify your database", and answering no blocked the change.

Version control is the other safeguard. Saved in the Power BI Project format (PBIP), a report and its model become folders of text files instead of one binary .pbix file. Commit those before the agent starts and its work arrives as an ordinary diff, which you can review and revert like any other change.

Whatever the agent reads, including table and column names and the rows returned by any query, is passed on to the AI provider behind your client, which may sit outside Microsoft's compliance commitments. For a model holding customer or payroll data, check that the provider is approved under your data policies before you connect.

An agent can draft and test a measure in minutes, and someone still has to read what comes back. For a developer new to Power BI, that takes enough DAX to see what a measure's filters will do in a table broken down by month or product, and enough knowledge of the business to confirm the agent used the right column and the right date. Writing the brief takes the same knowledge. Ours could only have come from someone who knew the model had an inactive relationship and which columns carried VAT.

These are data modelling and DAX skills, which many learners find the hardest part of Power BI. If the person reviewing agent output hasn't had that grounding yet, it helps to know how to judge whether a Power BI course covers enough DAX and data modelling, because courses vary a great deal in how much time they give to those two subjects compared with building charts.

Take a copy of a model you know well and connect the local server to it in read-only mode. Ask the agent to describe every table and measure in its own words, then check those descriptions against what you know the model does. Wherever the two differ, the model needs a clearer name or a written description, and the corrected text can go straight into the description fields. With those in place before you allow any writes, the agent starts its first real task with the same definitions your finance team uses.

#10652. Show, Don't Certify: How to Prepare for Your First Data Analyst Job

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Getting shortlisted for a junior data analyst job depends less on how many courses you have finished and more on what you can show. This plan breaks preparation into eight skill areas, pairs each one with a learning option and a hands-on task, and gives you a simple test for knowing when to move on.

Many people preparing for their first analyst role study for months and still struggle to get interviews. Their CVs list SQL, Excel and Tableau, yet nothing on the page shows those tools being used to answer a real question. Recruiters reading dozens of similar applications need something more concrete.

A course can give your learning a sensible order, but a completion badge says little about how you think. A small analysis that states its question, explains its method and admits its limits tells a hiring manager far more. This plan aims to collect that kind of proof while you learn, not after.

Start by collecting ten to twenty entry-level postings in the industry you want to join, and tally the tools and tasks they mention. You will quickly see a pattern. Some roles are built around Excel and routine reporting, while others want SQL, a BI platform and a bit of Python.

The wider market follows a similar shape. Jobright’s 2026 analysis of data analyst hiring, which looked at 2025–2026 postings from LinkedIn, Indeed and Levels.fyi, found that more than 80% of data analyst jobs mention SQL, over 60% mention Excel or Google Sheets, and roughly half mention Python or R. Let your own tally decide the order you learn in, but expect SQL to sit near the top.

As you work through each area below, finish with something you can show someone: a query, a tidied dataset, a chart, a one-page report or a portfolio project. Free material is often enough if you can stay disciplined. Pay for teaching only when it gives you something you cannot get alone, such as feedback, accountability or a fixed timetable.

SQL is how analysts pull, join and summarise records from a database, and a tool like Tableau lets other people explore what you found. Treat them as one workflow rather than two subjects: frame a question, write the query, sanity-check the numbers and present the result so a non-technical reader can follow it.

SQLBolt offers free, bite-sized SQL lessons that run in the browser, and Tableau publishes free training videos on connecting to data and building dashboards. Used together, they are enough to produce a small end-to-end project.

If you learn better with a set schedule and an instructor checking your work, you could learn to analyse data with SQL and Tableau from scratch through a guided programme at Heicoders Academy in Singapore. Whichever route you take, compare what is covered, how feedback is given, when sessions run and the full cost against what free resources already offer.

Proof you can show: A working SQL query set and your first published dashboard.

Move on when: You can take a question from idea to dashboard on your own, without following a tutorial line by line.

Spreadsheets are still where much of a team’s day-to-day reporting lives, especially when colleagues don’t write SQL. Get comfortable with filtering, pivot tables, and lookup functions, and learn to spot quiet errors like dates saved as text or one category spelled three different ways.

The next step is automating the cleanup so it doesn’t have to be repeated by hand each month. Microsoft’s Power Query documentation shows how to import and reshape data in recorded steps that refresh when new data arrives. The same ideas apply in other spreadsheet tools too.

Proof you can show: A messy export turned into a summary that refreshes itself.

Move on when: You can clean an untidy CSV and build a report that updates when the source file changes.

For most junior analyst roles, the technical interview is a SQL test. Basic SELECT statements won’t be enough; interviewers like to probe joins, subqueries, common table expressions and window functions, which answer everyday questions such as running totals or each customer’s first purchase.

Practise with timed problem sets and review your answers for correctness as well as speed.

Proof you can show: Confidence going into a live or take-home SQL test.

Move on when: You can solve intermediate problems in around 15 to 20 minutes each without looking up syntax.

Take-home assignments often come with data that has been made messy on purpose. Reviewers want to see how you deal with missing values, duplicates and inconsistent formats, and whether they can follow your reasoning afterwards.

Keep a short cleaning log as you work, noting each change and why you made it. A separate sheet tab or a list of comments in a notebook both work.

Proof you can show: A take-home submission a reviewer can audit step by step.

Move on when: Every change to your dataset is written down with a reason.

Case-study questions test whether you can read numbers sensibly, not just produce them. A working grasp of distributions, means and medians, spread, correlation and sampling will keep you away from the mistakes that undo otherwise good answers, such as quoting an average that a few extreme values have pulled out of shape.

Proof you can show: Clear, careful answers in case-study interviews.

Move on when: You can explain in everyday language why two things moving together doesn’t mean one causes the other.

Every chart should help someone answer a specific question. Learn where bars, lines, scatter plots and simple tables each work best, and how axis ranges, labels and colour can nudge a reader towards the wrong conclusion. On a dashboard, the most important finding should be the first thing people see.

Datawrapper’s guidance on picking chart types is a useful free reference. As practice, show the same result in two different chart types and decide which one represents the data more honestly.

Proof you can show: Portfolio charts that make a point, not just show off features.

Move on when: A person who has never seen your data can spot the main finding and its caveat at a glance.

What counts as a useful metric depends on the role. Marketing analysts focus on conversion and retention, operations analysts on delivery times and capacity, and finance analysts on margins and cash flow. Choose one sector and learn exactly how its key measures are defined.

Data.gov hosts a large, searchable collection of public datasets you can practise on for free. Before drawing any conclusions, read each dataset’s documentation, check when it was last updated and confirm its terms of use.

Proof you can show: A project built around the questions your target industry actually asks.

Move on when: You can define several sector metrics and describe how a manager would use each one.

Your portfolio is where the other seven areas come together. Two or three deep, well-documented projects will say more about you than a long list of quick ones. Give each project a README that sets out the question, the data, your method, the findings and the limitations.

Proof you can show: A portfolio link and a write-up you can talk through in an interview.

Move on when: Every project clearly explains what it asked, how you answered it and what you found.

There is no need to finish all eight areas before you apply. Once you can use the tools your target roles mention, speak honestly about your work and point to two or three clearly explained projects, start sending applications and keep learning alongside them.

Adjust each application to the job. Lead with a reporting example for spreadsheet-heavy roles, a query that solved a real problem for SQL roles, and a chart that supported a decision for visualisation roles. Be upfront about which work you built independently and which came from guided exercises.

Do I really need eight courses? No. These are skill areas, not eight required qualifications. A single resource can cover several of them, and practice you do on your own counts as much as classroom learning.

Is it possible to prepare using only free material? Yes. Free tutorials, open textbooks, official documentation and public datasets cover the fundamentals well. The harder parts are sticking to a routine and finding people who will give you honest feedback.

Which should come first, SQL or spreadsheets? It depends on the jobs you want. Spreadsheets are an easy starting point for reporting roles, while SQL is expected wherever databases are involved. You will want both eventually, so start with whichever your target postings stress most.

How many portfolio projects should I have? Two or three solid ones are a good start. Each should show a clear question, steps someone else could repeat and an honest reading of the results, rather than several near-identical dashboards.

Do I need a certificate before I apply? Not usually. Some employers ask for one, but many judge skills through interviews, take-home tasks and tests instead. Go by what each posting actually requires.

Security remains a top priority for the system. The Authentication Failure Lock , a security feature in case of theft, locks the screen after unsuccessful authentication attempts. #10646. This new chip survives 1300°F (700°C) and could change AI forever

Modern electronics power everything from smartphones to satellites, but they all share a major limitation. Heat. Once temperatures climb above roughly 200 degrees Celsius, most devices begin to break down. For decades, this thermal barrier has been one of the toughest challenges in engineering.

Researchers at the University of Southern California now believe they have found a way past that limit.

In a study published on March 26, 2026 in Science, a team led by Joshua Yang, Arthur B. Freeman Chair Professor at the Ming Hsieh Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the USC Viterbi School of Engineering and the USC School of Advanced Computing, unveiled a new type of memory device that continues to operate at 700 degrees Celsius (~1300 degrees Fahrenheit). That temperature exceeds molten lava and goes far beyond anything previously achieved for this class of technology. The device showed no sign of failure. In fact, 700 degrees was simply the maximum their equipment could test.

"You may call it a revolution," Yang said. "It is the best high-temperature memory ever demonstrated."

The new device is known as a memristor, a nanoscale component that can both store data and perform computations. It is constructed like a microscopic layered structure, with two electrodes on either side and a thin ceramic layer in between.

Jian Zhao, the study's first author, built the device using tungsten for the top electrode, hafnium oxide ceramic in the middle, and graphene for the bottom layer. Tungsten has the highest melting point of any element, while graphene, a single-atom-thick sheet of carbon, is known for its exceptional strength and heat resistance.

This combination produced remarkable performance. The device retained data for more than 50 hours at 700 degrees without needing to be refreshed. It also endured over one billion switching cycles at that temperature and operated at just 1.5 volts with speeds measured in tens of nanoseconds.

The discovery was not part of the team's original plan. They were initially attempting to create a different graphene-based device, which did not work as intended. Along the way, they encountered something surprising.

"To be honest, it was by accident, as most discoveries are," Yang said. "If you can predict it, it's usually not surprising, and probably not significant enough."

Further investigation revealed why the device performed so well. In conventional electronics, heat causes metal atoms in the top electrode to slowly migrate through the ceramic layer. Eventually, they reach the bottom electrode, creating a permanent connection that short-circuits the device and leaves it stuck in the on state.

Graphene prevents this failure. Its interaction with tungsten is, as Yang described it, similar to oil and water. Tungsten atoms that approach the graphene surface cannot attach to it. Without a stable point to settle, they drift away instead of forming a conductive bridge. This prevents short circuits and preserves the device's function even under extreme heat.

The researchers confirmed this mechanism using advanced electron microscopy, spectroscopy, and quantum-level simulations. By understanding what happens at the atomic interface, they have turned an unexpected result into a principle that could guide future designs. Other materials with similar surface properties could be identified, which may help scale the technology for industrial production.

Electronics capable of operating above 500 degrees Celsius have long been a goal for space exploration. Venus, for example, has a surface temperature around that level, and every lander sent there has failed in part due to extreme heat. Current silicon-based chips cannot survive such conditions.

"We are now above 700 degrees, and we suspect it will go higher," Yang said.

The potential applications go far beyond space missions. Geothermal energy systems require electronics that can function deep underground, where surrounding rock can glow red-hot. Nuclear and fusion systems also expose equipment to intense heat. Even in everyday settings, durability improves significantly. A device rated for 700 degrees would be extremely robust at the roughly 125-degree temperatures often reached inside automotive electronics.

Why It Matters for Artificial Intelligence

In addition to storing data, the device offers a major advantage for artificial intelligence. Many AI systems rely heavily on matrix multiplication, a mathematical operation used in tasks like image recognition and language processing. Traditional computers perform these calculations step by step, consuming large amounts of energy.

Memristors approach the problem differently. By using Ohm's Law, where voltage times conductance equals current, the device performs calculations directly as electricity flows through it. The result is obtained instantly as the measured current.

"Over 92 percent of the computing in AI systems like ChatGPT is nothing but matrix multiplication," Yang said. "This type of device can perform that in the most efficient way, orders of magnitude faster and at lower energy."

Yang and three co-authors of the study (Qiangfei Xia, Miao Hu, and Ning Ge) have already co-founded a company called TetraMem to commercialize memristor-based AI chips at room temperature. Their lab is already using working chips from TetraMem for machine learning tasks. The high-temperature version described in this research could extend those capabilities to environments where traditional electronics cannot operate, allowing devices such as spacecraft or industrial sensors to process data directly on site.

From Lab Prototype to Real-World Technology

Despite the promising results, Yang emphasizes that practical applications are still some distance away. Memory is only one part of a complete computing system. High-temperature logic circuits will also need to be developed and integrated. In addition, the current devices were built manually at very small scales in a laboratory setting, so manufacturing at scale will take time.

"This is the first step," Yang said. "It's still a long way to go. But logically, you can see: now it makes it possible. The missing component has been made."

From a manufacturing perspective, two of the materials used in the device, tungsten and hafnium oxide, are already widely used in semiconductor production. Graphene is newer but is actively being developed by major companies such as TSMC and Samsung, and it has already been produced at wafer scale in research environments.

The work was conducted through the CONCRETE Center, short for Center of Neuromorphic Computing under Extreme Environments, a multi-university Center of Excellence led by USC and supported by the Air Force Office of Scientific Research and the Air Force Research Laboratory. Key experimental work was carried out in collaboration with Dr. Sabyasachi Ganguli's team at the AFRL Materials Lab in Dayton, Ohio, while theoretical analysis involved USC researchers and collaborators at Kumamoto University in Japan.

For Yang, publication in Science reflects more than a single achievement.

"Space exploration has never been so real, so close, and at such a large scale," he said. "This paper represents a critical leap into a much larger, more exciting frontier."

#10644. This new chip could slash data center energy waste

As data centers consume more energy to support growing digital demands, engineers at the University of California San Diego have introduced a new chip design that could make powering graphics processing units (GPUs) more efficient. The innovation focuses on a key function in electronics: converting high voltages into the lower levels required by computing hardware. In laboratory testing, a prototype chip successfully performed this type of voltage conversion with high efficiency under conditions similar to those found in modern data centers.

The findings, published in Nature Communications, suggest the potential for smaller and more energy-efficient systems in advanced computing environments.

Rethinking DC-DC Converters for Modern Electronics

At the center of the new design is an improved version of a widely used component known as a DC-DC step-down converter. These converters are found in nearly all electronic devices and serve as a critical link between power sources and sensitive circuits. Their job is to take a high incoming voltage and reduce it to the exact level needed for safe operation.

In data centers, electricity is often distributed at 48 volts, while GPU processors typically require much lower voltages, usually between 1 and 5 volts. Efficiently managing this large voltage drop has become increasingly challenging as computing systems grow more powerful and compact.

Limits of Traditional Power Conversion Technology

Conventional step-down converters often struggle when dealing with large differences between input and output voltage. As that gap increases, efficiency drops and it becomes harder to supply enough current. Most existing designs rely on magnetic components such as inductors. While these components have been refined over many years, they are approaching their practical limits and are becoming harder to improve further.

"We've gotten so good at designing inductive converters that there's not really much room left to improve them to meet future needs," said study senior author Patrick Mercier, professor in the Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the UC San Diego Jacobs School of Engineering.

Exploring Piezoelectric Resonators as an Alternative

To move beyond these limitations, Mercier and his team, including first author Jae-Young Ko, an electrical and computer engineering Ph.D. student at UC San Diego, investigated a different approach using piezoelectric resonators. These small devices store and transfer energy through mechanical vibrations rather than magnetic fields.

Converters based on piezoelectric components could offer several advantages. They have the potential to be smaller, more energy dense, more efficient, and easier to manufacture at scale. "They have a lot of room to grow and have the potential to deliver better performance than anything that's come before them," Mercier said.

However, earlier versions of piezoelectric converters have had difficulty maintaining efficiency and delivering sufficient power when handling large voltage differences.

Hybrid Design Achieves High Efficiency and Power Output

To overcome these issues, the researchers created a hybrid converter that combines a piezoelectric resonator with small, commercially available capacitors arranged in a carefully designed configuration. This setup enables the system to handle larger voltage conversions more effectively.

The team incorporated this design into a prototype chip and tested its performance. The device successfully converted 48 volts down to 4.8 volts -- a level commonly required in data centers -- with a peak efficiency of 96.2 percent. It also delivered roughly four times more output current than previous piezoelectric-based designs.

This hybrid approach offers several benefits. It creates multiple pathways for energy to move through the system, reduces wasted power, and lessens the strain on the resonator. Together, these improvements enhance both efficiency and power delivery while only slightly increasing the chip's size.

Challenges and Next Steps for Real-World Use

Although the technology shows strong promise, it is still in the early stages of development. The researchers view it as an important step toward overcoming the constraints of current power conversion systems. Future efforts will focus on refining materials, improving circuit designs, and developing better packaging methods.

One challenge is that piezoelectric resonators physically vibrate, which means they cannot be attached to circuit boards using standard soldering techniques. New integration strategies will be needed to incorporate them into electronic systems, Mercier explained.

"Piezoelectric-based converters aren't quite ready to replace existing power converter technologies yet," Mercier added. "But they offer a trajectory for improvement. We need to continue to improve on multiple areas -- materials, circuits and packaging -- to make this technology ready for data center applications."

This project was supported in part by the Power Management Integration Center (PMIC), an Industry-University Cooperative Research Center (IUCRC) funded by the National Science Foundation (award number 2052809).

Materials provided by University of California - San Diego. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10641. What is Model Routing in AI?

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

"Do you know the closest ATM?" and "I was charged twice, the refund never arrived, and now my card is blocked" are both customer messages. Most AI apps send both to the same large model and pay large-model prices for each one.

Model routing fixes that. A router looks at each request and sends it to the cheapest model that can handle it, and keeps the expensive model for the requests that need it.

In this guide we'll cover what model routing is, how routers decide, and what it saves. Then we'll build a small router in about 60 lines of JavaScript and run it over 200 customer messages. At one setting it matched a large model's accuracy for about a third of the cost. At another it came within a point and a half for 89% less.

Model routing in AI is the practice of sending each request to the model best suited to it, instead of sending every request to one model. A router sits between your app and a pool of models, reads each request, and picks a model based on what you care about: cost, speed, or quality.

You'll also see it called AI model routing, LLM routing, or LLM model routing. They all mean the same thing. The pool might be a small and a large model from one provider, or models from several providers behind one API.

The idea rests on one observation: most requests are easy. A small model answers them as well as a large one, in a fraction of the time and for a fraction of the price. The router's whole job is finding the hard ones.

The diagram below shows a typical AI model routing architecture.

Model routing works in three steps: the router reads the request, decides which model should answer it, and forwards the request to that model. The middle step is where routers differ. Routing tools decide which model to use in one of four ways.

Rule-based routing uses rules you write by hand. Requests from free-plan users go to a small model. Anything tagged as code goes to a coding model. Prompts over a certain length go to a model with a bigger context window. It's simple, predictable, and free to run, but the rules only know what you thought of in advance.

Intelligent model routing uses a model to make the decision. A small, fast classifier reads each request and predicts which model in the pool will answer it well. You'll also see it called smart or dynamic model routing.

Two more terms come up here. Task-aware routing picks a model by the kind of job, like summarising, coding, or answering a support question. Context-aware routing also looks at what surrounds the request, like the conversation so far, the user, or the tools available.

The big platforms work this way. Microsoft describes its Foundry model router as "a trained language model" that picks a model for each prompt in real time. Google says Gemini Notebook uses its new Decisions API as a real-time router, serving everyday queries with up to 40% lower latency. I've tested the Google Decisions API against Jev on the same messages we'll use below.

Cascade routing tries the cheapest model first and only moves up when the answer isn't good enough. The usual test is confidence: if the cheap model is sure, keep its answer. If it isn't, send the request to a stronger model. It's also called confidence-based routing, and it's what we'll build below.

A cascade needs no separate classifier, because the first model's own confidence does the routing. The trade-off is that escalated requests pay for two models and wait for both.

Fallback routing is about staying up, not saving money. If a model times out, hits a rate limit, or its provider has an outage, the router sends the request to the next model on a list. Most production routers combine fallbacks with one of the three methods above.

The main benefits of model routing in AI systems are lower cost, faster responses, and fewer outages, without giving up quality on the hard requests.

We'll build a cascade router for a job every support tool has: sorting customer messages. Each message gets one of ten labels, like atm support or request refund.

The test set is 200 messages from Banking77, a public set of banking support messages: ten labels, 20 messages each. They're the same 200 messages from my zero shot classification test, which is where the single-model numbers below come from.

Put both keys in a .env file and run the examples with node --env-file=.env. It's the same setup as in my guide to getting a YouTube API key.

Create router.mjs. The first function sends the message to Jev with one multiple-choice question, and returns the label Jev picked along with its confidence:

Confidence runs from 0 to 1. When nearly all the probability sits on one label, it's close to 1. When Jev is torn between two labels, it drops.

The second function asks Claude Opus 5.5 the same question. The output_config block limits the answer to one of our labels, so there's no free text to parse. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema explains how that works.

The router itself is about a dozen lines:

If Jev is at least 80% confident, its answer goes straight back. If not, Claude answers instead. And if Claude fails, the router falls back to Jev's answer, because a shaky answer beats an error page.

Try it on an easy message and a harder one:

Jev was sure about the lost card. On the ATM question it leaned towards card acceptance with a confidence of 0.64, so the router asked Claude, which got it right.

Because cheap, strong, and threshold are options, the same function routes between any two models.

Model routing reduces AI costs by keeping easy requests on a cheap model and paying for the expensive one only when it's needed. Here's what that looked like on our 200 messages, starting with each model on its own:

Opus gets six more messages right out of 200, and costs more than 120 times as much.

Now the router, at four confidence thresholds:

Speed improved too. At the 0.8 threshold, the messages Jev kept came back in a median of 227 ms. The ones that escalated took about 2.1 seconds, because they waited for both models.

A note on method: I ran every message through both models once and saved the answers, then replayed the router over them at each threshold. That's how you compare thresholds without paying for the same test four times.

Then I ran the router live to check. At 0.95 it scored the same 95.0% and sent 61 messages to Opus. At 0.8 it scored 93.5% and sent 20, a point under the replay. Two answers moved: one of Jev's confidence scores landed at 0.82 instead of 0.78, just over the line, and Opus changed its answer on one message. Scores near the threshold shift a little between runs, so leave yourself a margin.

The choice of cheap model matters less than you'd think. With Google's Gemini Decision 1 in first place instead of Jev, a 0.7 threshold reached the same 95% for US$0.28 per 1,000 messages.

Model routing can cost a little quality, and the threshold decides how much. In our test the gap to Opus ranged from nothing at 0.95 to two points at 0.7.

The loss comes from one place: answers the cheap model got wrong while feeling sure. At the 0.8 threshold, Jev kept five wrong answers because its confidence was above the line. Opus got four of those five wrong as well, so only one was a real loss: "Do all ATMs take this card?", which Jev filed under card acceptance with a confidence of 0.91.

Escalating isn't risk-free either. On one message, Jev had the right label with a confidence of 0.44, and Opus overruled it with the wrong one.

To optimize costs using AI model routing, measure first, route second, and keep measuring. These six steps cover LLM model routing cost optimization for any pair of models:

The return is easy to estimate once you know the split. If nine in ten requests stay on a model that costs 1% as much, the bill drops by about 89%, which is what we measured.

Several inference providers have multi-model routing built in, and most AI gateway platforms support model routing too, so you don't have to write your own. As of November 2026, these are the top model routing solutions for AI apps. Each name links to the tool's model routing docs:

Hosted model routing platforms for large language models decide for you, which is the fastest way to start. A hand-built router like ours takes more work, but you choose the signal and you can see why every request went where it did.

An LLM router is the component that does model routing for large language models. It can be a function in your own code, like the one in this guide, a hosted model such as Microsoft Foundry's model router, or a feature of an AI gateway.

Smart model routing is another name for intelligent model routing: a model, not a hand-written rule, decides where each request goes. Dynamic model routing means the same thing.

The ROI of AI model routing depends on how much of your traffic a cheaper model can handle. In our test, a 0.8 threshold kept 89.5% of messages on the cheap model and cut the cost per 1,000 messages from US$1.96 to US$0.22, for about a point of accuracy. The build was about 60 lines of code.

OpenRouter's auto model routing works through its Auto Router. You set the model to openrouter/auto, and a decision model picks a model and a reasoning effort for each request. You pay the standard rate of whichever model it selects, with no extra fee.

OpenClaw model routing is a primary model plus an ordered list of fallbacks. In the OpenClaw model routing configuration you set agents.defaults.model.primary and agents.defaults.model.fallbacks, and an agent can override the fallback list. When a model fails, OpenClaw first rotates auth profiles on the same provider, then moves to the next model on the list, as its model failover docs describe. That makes it fallback routing, not cost-based routing.

An AI gateway simplifies model routing by putting every model behind one API, one set of keys, and one place for logs. Your app calls the gateway, and the routing rules live in config instead of code, so changing a model doesn't need a deploy.

Mid-stream failover is when a router switches models after a streamed response has already started, because the first model failed partway through. It's the hardest kind of failover, because the user has already seen part of an answer. Many routers only fail over before the first token arrives and return an error after that, so check what your gateway does.

#10638. “Giant superatoms” could finally solve quantum computing’s biggest problem

Researchers at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden have introduced a new theoretical design for quantum systems based on what they call "giant superatoms." This concept offers a fresh way to protect, control, and share quantum information, potentially bringing scientists closer to building large-scale quantum computers.

Quantum computers are expected to transform fields like drug discovery and encryption by solving problems that are far beyond the reach of conventional machines. However, progress has been limited by a major challenge known as decoherence. This occurs when quantum bits, or qubits, lose their information due to interactions with their surroundings. Even small amounts of electromagnetic noise can disrupt the fragile quantum states needed for computation.

"Quantum systems are extraordinarily powerful but also extremely fragile. The key to making them useful is learning how to control their interaction with the surrounding environment," says Lei Du, postdoctoral researcher in applied quantum technology at Chalmers.

Lei Du is the lead author of a study that outlines this new type of quantum system. The design is built around giant superatoms, which combine several important features. These systems reduce decoherence, remain stable, and consist of multiple interconnected "atoms" that function together as a single unit.

Giant superatoms bring together two previously separate ideas in quantum physics: giant atoms and superatoms. While each has been studied on its own, this is the first time they have been merged into a single system. These structures behave like atoms but are not found in nature. Instead, they are engineered by scientists (see fact box below).

The idea of giant atoms was first introduced by researchers at Chalmers over a decade ago and is now widely used in the field. A giant atom is typically designed as a qubit (which is the smallest unit of quantum information). Unlike ordinary atoms, it connects to light or sound waves at multiple, physically separated points. This allows it to interact with its environment in several places at once, helping it preserve quantum information.

"Waves that leave one connection point can travel through the environment and return to affect the atom at another point -- similar to hearing an echo of your own voice before you've finished speaking. This self-interaction leads to highly beneficial quantum effects, reduces decoherence and gives the system a form of memory of past interactions," explains Anton Frisk Kockum, Associate Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Although giant atoms have improved understanding of quantum behavior, they have had limitations when it comes to entanglement. Entanglement allows multiple qubits to share a single quantum state and act as one coordinated system, which is essential for powerful quantum computers.

To overcome this limitation, the research team combined giant atoms with the concept of superatoms. A superatom consists of several natural atoms that share the same quantum state and behave collectively as one larger atom.

This combination is expected to make it easier to create complex quantum states needed for quantum communication, networks, and highly sensitive measurement systems.

"A giant superatom may be envisaged as multiple giant atoms working together as a single entity, exhibiting a non-local interaction between light and matter. This enables quantum information from multiple qubits to be stored and controlled within one unit, without the need for increasingly complex surrounding circuitry," explains Lei Du.

"Giant superatoms open the door to entirely new capabilities, giving us a powerful new toolbox. They allow us to control quantum information and create entanglement in ways that were previously extremely difficult, or even impossible," says Janine Splettstoesser, Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Toward Scalable and Practical Quantum Systems

This work creates new possibilities for building quantum systems that are both scalable and reliable. The researchers plan to move from theory toward actually constructing these systems. Their design could also be integrated with other quantum technologies, serving as a building block for connecting different types of quantum platforms.

"There is currently strong interest in hybrid approaches, in which different quantum systems work together, because each has its own strengths," says Anton Frisk Kockum. "Our research shows that smart design can reduce the need for increasingly complex hardware and giant superatoms are bringing us one step closer to practically applicable quantum technology."

More on: Methods for protecting, controlling and distributing quantum information

The study shows that the way giant superatoms interact with light depends on their internal quantum states. This discovery gives researchers greater control over how quantum information moves through a system. They describe two different ways of connecting these structures to achieve useful outcomes.

In one setup, several giant superatoms are closely linked in a specific arrangement. This allows them to pass quantum states between each other without decoherence, meaning no information is lost.

In another setup, the atoms are spaced farther apart but connected in a carefully tuned way so that waves remain synchronized. This makes it possible to direct quantum signals and distribute entanglement over long distances.

Superatoms and giant atoms are engineered systems that behave like atoms rather than naturally occurring ones.

A superatom is a quantum system made up of multiple natural atoms that share a single quantum state and respond to light as one entity.

A giant atom, on the other hand, connects to light or sound waves at several separate points in space. It is called "giant" because it is larger than the wavelength of the light it interacts with.

Giant atoms have defined energy levels and follow the rules of quantum mechanics, yet they can reach sizes of up to millimeters, making them visible to the naked eye. Through electromagnetic or acoustic waves, they can interact with their surroundings at multiple locations at the same time. One way to picture this is as a single atom linked to a wave at several distant points. This unusual setup allows the atom to be influenced by the waves it produces.

Materials provided by Chalmers University of Technology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10637. Asynchronous Robot Inference: Decoupling Action Prediction and Execution

#10634. This simple change stops robot swarms from getting stuck

Imagine a swarm of robots rushing to complete an urgent job, such as cleaning up an oil spill or assembling complex machinery. At first, adding more robots speeds things up. But after a certain point, the space becomes crowded, robots start interfering with one another, and overall progress slows.

This raises a simple but important question: in a limited area, how many robots can you deploy before efficiency starts to drop? Researchers at Harvard believe they have found a clear answer.

A new study from the lab of L. Mahadevan, the Lola England de Valpine Professor of Applied Mathematics, Organismic and Evolutionary Biology, and Physics, shows that adding a controlled amount of randomness to how robots move can reduce congestion and improve performance in crowded environments.

The work combines mathematical modeling, computer simulations, and real-world experiments. It demonstrates how basic local movement rules can lead to organized, efficient outcomes on a larger scale. The findings could influence how robotic fleets are designed and may even apply to human crowd management and traffic flow. The research was published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences and led by applied mathematics Ph.D. student Lucy Liu, with guidance from SEAS Senior Research Fellow Justin Werfel.

Why Randomness Helps Predict Complex Behavior

Studying dense crowds is difficult because individuals can take countless possible paths and interact in unpredictable ways, Liu explained. To simplify the problem, the researchers treated each robot as a basic unit with a small, adjustable amount of variation in its movement.

"This might be counterintuitive, because how could randomness make things easier to work with?" said Liu. "But in this case, when you have a lot of randomness, it becomes possible to take averages -- average distances, average times, average behaviors. This makes it a lot easier to make predictions."

To explore this idea, the team created computer simulations of robot groups, referred to as agents. Each agent started at a random location and was assigned a random destination. Once it reached its target, it immediately received a new one, mimicking continuous task assignment in real-world systems.

Each agent moved toward its goal with a tunable amount of variation, described as "noise." With no noise, agents moved in straight lines. With high noise, their paths became erratic and inefficient. However, this wandering also helped them navigate around one another.

The simulations revealed a clear pattern. When agents moved in perfectly straight paths, they quickly formed dense clusters and traffic jams that halted progress. When movement became too random, congestion disappeared but efficiency dropped due to excessive wandering.

Between these extremes, the researchers identified a sweet spot. In this range, agents occasionally bumped into one another and formed short-lived clusters, but still managed to slip past and keep moving. This balance allowed the system to maintain a steady flow.

Using these insights, the team developed formulas to estimate "goal attainment rate," or how many destinations are reached over time. These equations made it possible to determine the ideal combination of crowd density and movement randomness to maximize performance.

To confirm their findings, Liu collaborated with physicist Federico Toschi at Eindhoven University of Technology in the Netherlands. Together, they set up experiments with small wheeled robots in a lab equipped with an overhead camera.

Each robot carried a QR code so its position could be tracked and updated with new destinations. Although the physical robots moved more slowly and less precisely than the simulated agents, they displayed the same overall patterns.

The experiments supported a key idea: highly complex coordination does not require advanced intelligence or centralized control. Instead, simple local rules can produce effective group behavior, at least within certain density limits.

"Understanding how active matter, whether it is a swarm of ants, a herd of animals, or a group of robots, become functional and execute tasks in crowded environments using the principles of self-organization, is relevant to many questions in behavioral ecology," Mahadevan said. "Our study suggests strategies that might well be much broader than the instantiation we have focused on."

Liu noted that she has long been interested in designing safer and more efficient crowded spaces. This research points toward a future where the movement of large groups, whether robots, vehicles, or people, could be predicted and optimized using mathematical tools.

The results suggest that introducing controlled variability into movement patterns may improve flow in many real-world systems, from factory floors to city streets.

Funding for the research came from the National Science Foundation Graduate Research Fellowship Program under Grant No. DGE 2140743, along with grants from the Simons Foundation and the Henri Seydoux Fund.

Materials provided by Harvard John A. Paulson School of Engineering and Applied Sciences. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
